Abstract: This paper presents a field-oriented methodology for modeling and controlling pressure regulation, demonstrated on a real water 
­
distribution network (WDN) sector. The aim is to provide a practical and reproducible pathway from field measurements to deployable real-
­
time pressure control, addressing the gap between simulator-based numerical studies and full-scale field implementations. The study covers 
­
data-driven dynamic modeling, controller design, and pre-deployment performance assessment using real operational data. A low-order 
­
linear dynamic model with transport delay is identified directly from field measurements, combining 20 days of pressure and inflow data 
­
with step-response tests performed on an existing pressure-reducing valve (PRV). The model is validated against an independent 24-h 
­
dataset of real consumption, achieving a maximum deviation of approximately ±1 m H2O at the remote critical node, which is adequate for 
­
control-oriented modeling and controller synthesis. Based on the identified model, a digital reference signal tracking (RST) controller with 
­
a Smith predictor is designed using explicit performance specifications for settling time, overshoot, and bandwidth. The controller is evalu-
­
ated in silico using real consumption data not used during identification, providing an unbiased assessment of closed-loop performance 
­
under representative demand variability. Results show that the proposed strategy maintains pressure at the remote critical node within 
­
acceptable bounds while effectively rejecting demand-induced disturbances. Although closed-loop field implementation is beyond the scope 
­
of this study, the framework is explicitly conceived as a preparatory step for real-time deployment. The main conclusion is that a transpar-
­
ent, data-driven methodology grounded in field measurements can reduce modeling and tuning effort while yielding controller designs 
­
compatible with standard PLC/SCADA platforms, enabling utilities to progress systematically toward practical real-time pressure control. 
­
DOI: 10.1061/JWRMD5.WRENG-7321. © 2026 American Society of Civil Engineers.
­
Practical Applications: This study offers practical guidance for water utilities seeking to improve pressure management while reducing 
­
water losses and infrastructure wear. Using real operational data, the proposed approach shows how effective pressure regulation strategies 
­
can be derived directly from field measurements and implemented with standard PLC and SCADA systems already used by most utilities. 
­
As a result, the methodology is accessible without requiring advanced hydraulic-modeling expertise or specialized simulation tools. Pressure 
­
management is particularly important because daily demand variations often produce excessive pressures during low consumption periods 
­
(e.g., nighttime). These pressure surges increase the likelihood of pipe bursts, background leakage, and premature deterioration of network 
­
assets. The proposed control strategy addresses this issue by stabilizing pressure at a critical point in the network, allowing utilities to maintain 
­
pressures within acceptable and regulatory limits despite changing demand. While the controller has not yet been deployed in closed-loop 
­
field operation, the methodology is designed as a practical step toward real-time implementation. By reducing modeling effort and simplifying 
­
controller tuning, the approach lowers technical barriers for utilities interested in advanced pressure control and provides a realistic foundation 
­
for improving network reliability and reducing operational costs.
­
Introduction
­
Water utilities must deliver potable water at adequate flow and 
­
pressure while minimizing water losses, operational costs, and asset 
­
deterioration. Modern water distribution networks (WDNs) exhibit 
­
pronounced intraday and seasonal demand variability, leading to 
­
large fluctuations in service pressure (Bello et al. 2019; Fan et al. 
­
2017). When pressures exceed acceptable limits—particularly dur-
­
ing low-demand periods—background leakage increases, pipe fail-
­
ure risk rises, and infrastructure lifetime is reduced (Van Zyl et al. 
­
2008; Page 2018). Stabilizing and reducing pressure is therefore 
­
among the most effective strategies to mitigate leakage and bursts, 
­
with substantial long-term economic benefits (Vicente et al. 2016; 
­
Creaco and Walski 2017).
­
In practice, pressure management typically relies on pressure-
­
reducing valves (PRVs) operated under relatively simple control 
­
schemes (Bello et al. 2019; Campisano et al. 2010; Creaco et al. 
­
2019; Galuppini et al. 2019). A common solution is the mechan‐
­
ically piloted PRV with a fixed downstream setpoint (Doghri 
­
et al. 2022; Ferrarese and Malavasi 2022). More advanced 
­
electronic add-on controllers modulate outlet pressure using 
­
predefined day–night profiles (time-based control) or as a func‐
­
tion of inlet flow (flow-modulated control) to approximately 
­
maintain acceptable pressures at remote locations using surrogate 
­
1
© ASCE
J. Water Resour. Plann. Manage.
Case Study
1
23
4
7
signals (Campisano et al. 2016; Fontana et al. 2018b; Galuppini 
­
et al. 2019). Remote real-time control extends these concepts 
­
by telemetering pressure at one or more critical nodes and con‐
­
tinuously adjusting PRV setpoints; laboratory and field studies 
­
report effective leakage reduction and improved pressure stabil‐
­
ity (Campisano et al. 2016; Fontana et al. 2018a, b; Galuppini 
­
et al. 2019). Comparative studies further confirm that remote-
­
node–based regulation typically outperforms fixed, time-based, 
­
and flow-modulated strategies in pressure stability and leakage 
­
reduction, albeit with increased sensing and communication 
­
requirements (Koşucu and Demirel 2024).
­
Motivated by the limitations of fixed-pressure and open-loop 
­
modulation, research has increasingly focused on real-time control 
­
(RTC) strategies that continuously adjust PRV outlet pressure based 
­
on measured network conditions. Early numerical and laboratory 
­
investigations demonstrated the potential of RTC to reduce average 
­
pressure and leakage (Campisano et al. 2010, 2012; Creaco and 
­
Franchini 2013). Subsequent studies introduced calibration meth-
­
ods for proportional or flow-dependent controllers (Campisano 
­
et al. 2012; Page et al. 2017; Page and Creaco 2019) and explored 
­
RTC architectures for practical deployment (Campisano et al. 2016; 
­
Fontana et al. 2018a, b). Reviews identify RTC as a promising 
­
direction but emphasize the need for methodologies that integrate 
­
model development, controller design, and practical implementa-
­
tion guidance (Vicente et al. 2016; Creaco et al. 2019). Recent work 
­
also highlights the importance of pressure regulation strategies that 
­
explicitly target leakage reduction under low-demand conditions, 
­
such as nighttime pressure management (Denardi et al. 2024).
­
The dynamic behavior of PRVs has also been studied exten-
­
sively. Prescott and Ulanicki (2003) showed that PRVs exhibit 
­
strongly nonlinear dynamics, and that linear simplifications can 
­
miss important behaviors, particularly near low valve openings. 
­
More recent studies characterized PRV responses under varying 
­
operating conditions and control modes (Doghri et al. 2022; 
­
Ferrarese and Malavasi 2022), while detailed analyses showed 
­
that electronically actuated PRVs may become unstable when 
­
valve–network gain increases sharply at small openings or when 
­
sampling is not properly selected (Janus and Ulanicki 2018, 2021). 
­
These findings align with work emphasizing the need for controller-
­
oriented dynamic models that explicitly account for delay and gain 
­
variation when PRVs act as feedback-control actuators (Ulanicki 
­
and Beaujean 2021).
­
At the same time, the availability of telemetry, remote actuators, 
­
and wireless communications has enabled a range of industrial 
­
pressure-control solutions. In many utilities, PRVs still operate 
­
in fixed-pressure mode (Doghri et al. 2022). Commercial retro-
­
fit controllers offer scheduled, flow-based, adaptive, and closed-
­
loop modes with telemetry and remote configuration (Technolog 
­
Limited 2015, 2017; i2O Water Ltd. 2012, 2013; BERMAD Water 
­
Technologies 2023; Cla-Val Company 2021), demonstrating the 
­
maturity of RTC technologies in operational settings.
­
Despite their effectiveness, commercial controllers are often 
­
configured through engineering judgment and heuristic tuning. Pro-
­
prietary algorithms typically do not expose PRV–network dynam-
­
ics, transport delays, or remote pressure interactions, limiting the 
­
ability to assess stability margins or predict closed-loop behavior 
­
under varying conditions (Creaco et al. 2019; Janus and Ulanicki 
­
2018; Galuppini et al. 2020a). This motivates transparent, reprodu-
­
cible, model-based methodologies that support systematic tuning, 
­
predictable performance, and implementation on standard PLC/
­
SCADA platforms.
­
More advanced schemes, including model predictive con-
­
trol, optimization-based approaches, and learning-based methods, 
­
have recently been proposed for multinode pressure regulation 
­
(Galuppini et al. 2023a; Castelletti et al. 2023; Denardi et al. 2024). 
­
Reinforcement-learning–based strategies combined with hydraulic 
­
simulation further illustrate the potential of data-driven control 
­
under uncertainty, albeit at the expense of increased modeling com-
­
plexity, computational effort, and limited transparency (Belfadil 
­
et al. 2024). Similarly, recent work has explored integrated pressure 
­
management, energy recovery, and economic optimization frame-
­
works (Hammond et al. 2024; Maio et al. 2024). While promising, 
­
these approaches are often difficult to deploy within the constraints 
­
of typical district metered areas (DMAs), where pressure regulation 
­
is performed by a single PRV, and implementation simplicity, 
­
robustness, and interpretability remain critical.
­
Over the last few years, controller-oriented modeling and dig-
­
ital design/tuning workflows that explicitly leverage WDN meas-
­
urements have been developed. In particular, Galuppini et al. 
­
(2022) proposed an in situ identification procedure that estimates 
­
frequency responses from measured input–output data via Black-
­
man–Tukey spectral analysis and then fits parametric transfer-
­
function models (including delay), specifically addressing the 
­
presence of colored exogenous disturbances. Complementarily, 
­
sum-of-delay gray-box structures have been introduced to represent 
­
the plant as a superposition of pure-delay contributions; due to the 
­
large number of candidate delays, sparse estimation (e.g., LASSO) 
­
can be used to reduce model complexity while preserving high-
­
frequency features such as resonance peaks that are relevant for 
­
regulator design (Galuppini et al. 2021). On the controller side, 
­
optimization-based tuning frameworks have been proposed to 
­
select digital regulator parameters under explicit robustness/per-
­
formance constraints, including stability margin indicators and 
­
sampling/discretization-related limits (Galuppini et al. 2020b).
­
However, despite these advances, few case studies report a 
­
complete and reproducible measurement-to-controller workflow 
­
on a real hydraulic sector using routine operational data and an 
­
implementation-ready control structure for standard industrial plat-
­
forms. This work addresses this gap by proposing a complete, 
­
data-driven, experimentally grounded methodology for modeling 
­
and regulating pressure at a remote critical node in a real hydraulic 
­
sector. Although the controller is validated numerically rather 
­
than in closed-loop field operation, the methodology relies on 
­
measurements from the real network rather than on synthetic 
­
models. Specifically:
­
•
A 20-day historical dataset of pressure and inflow is used to 
­
derive a stationary nonlinear relationship between flow and head 
­
losses between the PRV and the remote critical node.
­
•
Step-response experiments on the existing PRV are used to 
­
identify a low-order linear dynamic model with a delay between 
­
the normalized pilot downstream setpoint, xp(t), and the remote-
­
node pressure head, hm(t).
­
•
The model is validated against an independent 24-h dataset 
­
of real consumption, reproducing critical node pressure within 
­
±1 m H2O.
­
This decomposition into a static nonlinear demand–headloss 
­
relation and a dynamic linear time-invariant (LTI) model yields 
­
a tractable representation suitable for controller design and 
­
deployment on standard industrial hardware, without requiring a 
­
hydraulic solver.
­
Compared with previous approaches, this work differs in three 
­
ways: (1) the dynamic model used for controller synthesis is 
­
identified from real measurements rather than a simulator; (2) the 
­
resulting low-order model is explicitly tailored for PLC/SCADA 
­
implementation; and (3) validation uses real consumption scenarios 
­
rather than artificially generated demand patterns. In this sense, 
­
the proposed methodology complements field studies such as 
­
Fontana et al. (2018b): while their focus is field implementation, 
­

Modern water distribution networks (WDNs) exhibit \\r\\n­\\r\\npronounced intraday and seasonal demand variability, leading to \\r\\n­\\r\\nlarge fluctuations in service pressure (Bello et al. 2019; Fan et al. \\r\\n­\\r\\n2017). Comparative studies further confirm that remote-\\r\\n­\\r\\nnode–based regulation typically outperforms fixed, time-based, \\r\\n­\\r\\nand flow-modulated strategies in pressure stability and leakage \\r\\n­\\r\\nreduction, albeit with increased sensing and communication \\r\\n­\\r\\nrequirements (Koşucu and Demirel 2024).\\r\\n­\\r\\nMotivated by the limitations of fixed-pressure and open-loop \\r\\n­\\r\\nmodulation, research has increasingly focused on real-time control \\r\\n­\\r\\n(RTC) strategies that continuously adjust PRV outlet pressure based \\r\\n­\\r\\non measured network conditions. Early numerical and laboratory \\r\\n­\\r\\ninvestigations demonstrated the potential of RTC to reduce average \\r\\n­\\r\\npressure and leakage (Campisano et al. 2010, 2012; Creaco and \\r\\n­\\r\\nFranchini 2013). While promising, \\r\\n­\\r\\nthese approaches are often difficult to deploy within the constraints \\r\\n­\\r\\nof typical district metered areas (DMAs), where pressure regulation \\r\\n­\\r\\nis performed by a single PRV, and implementation simplicity, \\r\\n­\\r\\nrobustness, and interpretability remain critical.\\r\\n­\\r\\nOver the last few years, controller-oriented modeling and dig-\\r\\n­\\r\\nital design/tuning workflows that explicitly leverage WDN meas-\\r\\n­\\r\\nurements have been developed. This work addresses this gap by proposing a complete, \\r\\n­\\r\\ndata-driven, experimentally grounded methodology for modeling \\r\\n­\\r\\nand regulating pressure at a remote critical node in a real hydraulic \\r\\n­\\r\\nsector. Although the controller is validated numerically rather \\r\\n­\\r\\nthan in closed-loop field operation, the methodology relies on \\r\\n­\\r\\nmeasurements from the real network rather than on synthetic \\r\\n­\\r\\nmodels. Specifically:\\r\\n­\\r\\n•\\r\\nA 20-day historical dataset of pressure and inflow is used to \\r\\n­\\r\\nderive a stationary nonlinear relationship between flow and head \\r\\n­\\r\\nlosses between the PRV and the remote critical node.\\r\\n­\\r\\n•\\r\\nStep-response experiments on the existing PRV are used to \\r\\n­\\r\\nidentify a low-order linear dynamic model with a delay between \\r\\n­\\r\\nthe normalized pilot downstream setpoint, xp(t), and the remote-\\r\\n­\\r\\nnode pressure head, hm(t).\\r\\n­\\r\\n•\\r\\nThe model is validated against an independent 24-h dataset \\r\\n­\\r\\nof real consumption, reproducing critical node pressure within \\r\\n­\\r\\n±1 m H2O.\\r\\n­\\r\\nThis decomposition into a static nonlinear demand–headloss \\r\\n­\\r\\nrelation and a dynamic linear time-invariant (LTI) model yields \\r\\n­\\r\\na tractable representation suitable for controller design and \\r\\n­\\r\\ndeployment on standard industrial hardware, without requiring a \\r\\n­\\r\\nhydraulic solver.\\r\\n­\\r\\nCompared with previous approaches, this work differs in three \\r\\n­\\r\\nways: (1) the dynamic model used for controller synthesis is \\r\\n­\\r\\nidentified from real measurements rather than a simulator; (2) the \\r\\n­\\r\\nresulting low-order model is explicitly tailored for PLC/SCADA \\r\\n­\\r\\nimplementation; and (3) validation uses real consumption scenarios \\r\\n­\\r\\nrather than artificially generated demand patterns. Tsinghua University\nL24: ## Description\nL25: Drinking Water Distribution Networks (DWDN) are susceptible to infrastructure failures, which may lead to water losses. Typically, these water losses are due to background leakages and pipe bursts which may occur anywhere within the distribution network. Background leakages are normally difficult to detect due to their small size, whereas pipe bursts are easier to locate as they are of larger size and may appear on the surface.\nL26: The early detection and localization of some leakage event is extremely important, as this would reduce the time required for accommodating the event and therefore reducing the risk of further infrastructure degradation, contamination events and consumer complaints.\nL27: In previous years, a number of methodologies have been proposed to detect and isolate the location of leakage events using various types of sensor measurements. These methods were commonly evaluated on private commercial datasets, and as a result, it is not possible to objectively compare these methods in their ability to detect and isolate leaks.\nL28: In the past year, a leakage detection dataset has been proposed, LeakDB, based on benchmark networks and created using the WNTR tool, using pressure-driven demands and realistic leakage modelling. Inspired by the “BATtle of the Attack Detection ALgorithms” (BATADAL), which focused on the detection of cyber-physical attacks, our team decided to organize a similar “battle” focusing on leakage events.\nL29: The Battle of the Leakage Detection and Isolation Methods (BattLeDIM),  aims at objectively comparing the performance of methods for the detection and localization of leakage events, relying on SCADA measurements of flow and pressure sensors installed within water distribution networks. Participants may use different types of tools and methods, including (but not limited to) engineering judgement, machine learning, statistical methods, signal processing, and model-based fault diagnosis approaches.\nL30: ## Files\nL31: \nL32: ###\nL33: \nL34: 2018_Fixed_Leakages_Report.txt\nL35: \nL36: cite11†Preview L37: ###\nL38: \nL39: Files (550.5 MB)\nL40: Name  | Size  |\nL41: --- November 2016\nL15: ### Development data\nL16: \nL17: The following data on hourly historical SCADA operations are provided:\nL18:   * Training Dataset 1: This dataset was released on November 20 2016, and it was generated from a one-year long simulation. The dataset does not contain any attacks, i.e. all the data pertains to C-Town normal operations.\nL19:   * Training Dataset 2: This dataset with partially labeled data was released on November 28 2016. The dataset is around 6 months long and contains several attacks, some of which are approximately labeled.\nL20:   * Test Dataset: This 3-months long dataset contains several attacks but no labels. The dataset was released on February 20 2017, and it is used to compare the performance of the algorithms (see rules document for details).\nL21: Note: the flow data unit is LPS, pressure and water level units are meters.\nL22: cite7†C-Town .inp file for EPANET L23: \nL24: cite8†Training Dataset 1 L25: \nL26: cite9†Training Dataset 2 cite10†List of attacks in Training Dataset 2 L27: \nL28: cite11†Test Dataset cite12†List of attacks in Test Dataset L29: ##### Reference\nL30: \nL31: If you are using the datasets in your work, please cite the following paper as reference:\nL32: Riccardo Taormina and Stefano Galelli and Nils Ole Tippenhauer and Elad Salomons and Avi Ostfeld and Demetrios G. Arango-Martinez}}\n+\\setlength{\\parindent}{0pt}\n+\\setlength{\\parskip}{5pt}\n+\\pagestyle{empty}\n+\\newcommand{\\datasetheading}[1]{\\vspace{5pt}\\textbf{\\large #1}\\par}\n+\n+\\begin{document}\n+{\\small DS675 \\hfill A. Arango-Martinez}\\par\n+{\\Large\\bfseries Milestone 1: Proposed Datasets}\\par\n+{\\normalsize Pressure Prediction in Drinking-Water Distribution Systems}\\par\n+\\vspace{2pt}\\hrule\\vspace{5pt}\n+\n+\\textbf{Area of interest.}\n+My interest is predicting pressure at remote or critical locations in drinking-water distribution networks. This builds on my previous work on remote-node pressure regulation using field measurements. For this project, I propose supervised regression using current and past SCADA measurements to predict future pressure. Both candidates below contain simulated network data and support a comparison of learning methods beyond exploratory analysis.\n+\n+\\datasetheading{1. BattLeDIM: L-Town}\n+\\textbf{Sources:}\n+\\href{https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/larserikrisholm/battledim}{Kaggle dataset}\n+\\enspace\\textbar\\enspace\n+\\href{https://zenodo.org/records/4017659}{Official dataset and documentation (Zenodo)}.\n+\n+\\textbf{Description.}\n+BattLeDIM was developed to evaluate leakage detection and localization in the simulated L-Town network. It provides measurements for 2018 and 2019 at five-minute intervals: 105,120 time observations per year, or 210,240 in total. Its 119 measurement channels comprise 33 pressures, three flows, one tank level, and 82 automated meter readings of consumption. These are numerical time-series variables. Network topology and leakage-event information are also available.\n+\n+\\textbf{Proposed task.}\n+Predict pressure at a selected monitored node \\textbf{15 minutes ahead}. Potential inputs include current and past pressures, flows, tank level, consumption, and time information. The target is a continuous pressure value, so this is regression rather than classification. The node would be selected after reviewing network topology and pressure conditions, with preference for a location relevant to critical-node pressure management.\n+\n+\\textbf{Why consider it?}\n+Its measurement frequency and distributed sensors closely match my engineering interests, and it has substantially more observations than variables. The original benchmark offers documented prior work on leakage detection; pressure forecasting provides a different project focus. A possible later extension is to examine whether forecast errors change during leakage events.\n+\n+\\datasetheading{2. BATADAL: C-Town}\n+\\textbf{Sources:}\n+\\href{https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/minhbtnguyen/batadal-a-dataset-for-cyber-attack-detection}{Kaggle dataset}\n+\\enspace\\textbar\\enspace\n+\\href{https://www.batadal.net/data.html}{Official dataset and documentation}.\n+\n+\\textbf{Description.}\n+BATADAL was developed for cyberattack detection in the simulated C-Town network. The hourly data include 8,761 normal-operation records, 4,177 records in a second development set, and 2,089 test records: 15,027 in total. Its 43 SCADA variables comprise seven tank levels, 12 flows, 12 actuator states, and 12 pressures. Measurements are numerical; actuator states are discrete. Attack information is available separately, with incomplete labeling in the original development data.\n+\n+\\textbf{Proposed task.}\n+Predict pressure at a selected monitored network location \\textbf{one hour ahead}, using current and past tank levels, pump and valve states, flows, and pressures. The target is continuous pressure; attack labels would not be input features or the primary target. Node selection would follow a review of the network and available pressure measurements.\n+\n+\\textbf{Why consider it?}\n+The combination of hydraulic measurements and actuator states supports studying how network operation relates to future pressure. Its size is manageable and considerably exceeds its feature count. Existing attack-detection work provides background for a different focus on pressure forecasting, potentially followed by analysis under abnormal conditions. Hourly sampling suits operational prediction, rather than fast valve-control dynamics.\n+\n+\\vspace{3pt}\n+\\textbf{Scope.}\n+These are two alternatives; the final dataset remains to be selected. Detailed feature selection and modeling decisions would follow in later milestones. Evaluation would preserve time order and use only information available when each forecast is made.\n+\\end{document}\n*** End Patch"));
text(await tools.exec_command({cmd:"New-Item -ItemType Directory -Force -Path 'build' | Out-Null; pdflatex -interaction=nonstopmode -halt-on-error -output-directory=build Milestone1.tex",workdir:"D:\\GitHub\\ML NJIT\\Kaggle\\Proyecto",yield_time_ms:1000,max_output_tokens:1400}));

Hourly sampling suits operational prediction, rather than fast valve-control dynamics.\n\n\\vspace{3pt}\n\\textbf{Scope.}\nThese are two alternatives; the final dataset remains to be selected. Detailed feature selection and modeling decisions would follow in later milestones. Evaluation would preserve time order and use only information available when each forecast is made.\n\\end{document}\n"

````text
---
name: "pdf"
description: "Read, create, inspect, render, and verify PDF files where visual layout matters, including fillable AcroForms. Use Poppler rendering plus Python tools such as reportlab, pdfplumber, and pypdf for generation and extraction."
---

# PDF Skill

## When To Use

- Read or review PDF content where layout and visuals matter.
- Create PDFs programmatically with reliable formatting.
- Fill and validate interactive PDF forms.
- Validate final rendering before delivery.

## Tools + Contract Requirements

Immediately before the first create/edit authoring command, run `mark_artifact_operation_started.mjs` successfully exactly once using the command below. Do not run it for read-only work. For edits, replace `create` with `edit`; adjust the expected count and output format to match the requested outputs.

```bash
node container_tools/mark_artifact_operation_started.mjs --operation-kind create --expected-output-count 1 --output-format pdf
```

## Workflow

1. Prefer visual review: render PDF pages to PNGs and inspect them.
   - Use `pdftoppm` from the bundled runtime or system Poppler when available.
   - If unavailable, install Poppler or ask the user to review the output locally.
2. Use `reportlab` to generate PDFs when creating new documents.
3. Use `pdfplumber` or `pypdf` for text extraction and quick checks; do not rely on text extraction for layout fidelity.
4. After each meaningful update, re-render pages and verify alignment, spacing, and legibility.

## Fill And Validate AcroForms

Visual review alone is not a correctness check for a fillable PDF. A page `/Widget` annotation can render a value from its appearance stream while the canonical `/AcroForm/Fields` tree is missing or contains a stale value.

1. Keep the result interactive by default; set `flatten=True` only when the user explicitly requests a completed, static form. Preserve the source PDF, and do not flatten a signed PDF without an explicit workflow decision.
2. Inspect both representations before filling: enumerate fields from `reader.get_fields()` and `/Widget` annotations from every page's `/Annots`, following `/Parent` and `/Kids`. If a widget and a canonical field have the same name but are distinct objects with no `/Parent` relationship, do not call `reattach_fields()` blindly: it can create a second top-level field with the same name. Report the ambiguity or produce a static result.
3. Recover genuinely orphaned widgets, fill all pages, and write the result with `pypdf`:

```python
from pypdf import PdfReader, PdfWriter
from pypdf.generic import NameObject

reader = PdfReader(input_pdf)
writer = PdfWriter()
writer.clone_document_from_reader(reader)

# Restores widgets that are missing from /AcroForm/Fields.
writer.reattach_fields()
fields = writer.get_fields() or {}
missing = set(expected_values) - set(fields)
if missing:
    raise ValueError(f"Form fields not found after repair: {sorted(missing)}")

values_to_write = dict(expected_values)
if flatten:
    # Paint every existing value before removing every widget.
    values_to_write = {
        name: field.get("/V", "/Off" if field.get("/FT") == "/Btn" else "")
        for name, field in fields.items()
    }
    values_to_write.update(expected_values)

writer.update_page_form_field_values(
    None, values_to_write, auto_regenerate=False, flatten=flatten
)

if flatten:
    # pypdf's flatten=True paints appearances but does not remove widgets.
    writer.remove_annotations(subtypes="/Widget")
    writer.root_object.pop(NameObject("/AcroForm"), None)

with open(output_pdf, "wb") as stream:
    writer.write(stream)
```

4. Reopen the written PDF before delivery. For an interactive result, require every expected field to be present in `get_fields()` with the expected `/V`, enumerate page widgets again, and confirm their effective `/V` (the widget value or inherited `/Parent` value) agrees. Confirm each updated widget has a non-empty `/AP` `/N` appearance and render the final pages to catch stale or clipped appearances. Do not rely on `/NeedAppearances` or a successful PNG render as proof that logical field data was updated.
5. For a flattened result, require zero `/Widget` annotations and no remaining `/AcroForm` field tree after reopening, then render the final pages. Keep an editable copy when the user may need to revise the form.

## Temp And Output Conventions

- Use `tmp/pdfs/` for intermediate files; delete them when done.
- Write final artifacts under `output/pdf/` when working in this repo.
- Keep filenames stable and descriptive.

## Dependencies

Prefer the Codex bundled workspace/runtime dependencies when available. The primary runtime is expected to include:

- Python packages: `reportlab`, `pdfplumber`, `pypdf`
- Rendering tools: `pdftoppm` and `pdfinfo` from Poppler

If a dependency is missing, install only what is needed.

Python packages:

```bash
uv pip install reportlab pdfplumber pypdf
```

If `uv` is unavailable:

```bash
python3 -m pip install reportlab pdfplumber pypdf
```

System tools for rendering:

```bash
# macOS (Homebrew)
brew install poppler

# Ubuntu/Debian
sudo apt-get install -y poppler-utils
```

If installation is not possible in this environment, tell the user which dependency is missing and how to install it locally.

## Environment

No required environment variables.

## Rendering Command

```bash
pdftoppm -png "$INPUT_PDF" "$OUTPUT_PREFIX"
```

## Quality Expectations

- Maintain polished visual design: consistent typography, spacing, margins, and section hierarchy.
- Avoid rendering issues: clipped text, overlapping elements, broken tables, black squares, or unreadable glyphs.
- Charts, tables, and images must be sharp, aligned, and clearly labeled.
- Use ASCII hyphens only. Avoid U+2011 and other Unicode dashes.
- Citations and references must be human-readable; never leave tool tokens or placeholder strings.

## Final Checks

- Do not deliver until the latest PNG inspection shows zero visual or formatting defects.
- Confirm headers, footers, page numbering, and section transitions look polished.
- Keep intermediate files organized or remove them after final approval.

## Final response

### Final response citations

Place `:codex-file-citation{...}` inline in prose, not in a trailing list. Use `purpose="source"` for Q&amp;A/no-op and `purpose="output"` for create/edit.

- [HARD REQUIREMENT] Create/edit: cite each final PDF exactly once with a plain output citation. Summarize representative changes; do not cite every page or add a separate filename, path, or Markdown link. Example: `Created :codex-file-citation{path="/abs/path/report.pdf" purpose="output"}, with the completed analysis and appendix.`
- Q&amp;A/no-op: do not edit or re-export. Inspect the complete relevant pages, preserve material headings, table/figure labels, footnotes, sources, and sample sizes, and cite each source PDF once with a plain source citation.

PDF citations currently support only plain file citations. Do not add `artifact_kind`, `page_number`, or other locators. Never cite rendered PNGs, scratch files, builders, or QA intermediates unless asked.

### Final response suggested followups

- After successfully creating, editing, or analyzing an artifact, finish your final response with 3 useful next actions tailored to the artifact and the user's goal. Completing the requested work is not a reason to skip these actions: they help the user continue from the result. Do not perform the actions unless the user chooses one.
- Each action must be a distinct, concrete request the user could send next. Do not repeat completed work or invent filler. Omit the list if the user declines suggestions, the requested work fails, or no genuinely useful next action remains.
- Present each action as an unescaped Markdown list item using this exact syntax: `- :codex-followup[Short action]{prompt="Complete request for that action"}`.
\documentclass[11pt,letterpaper]{article}
\usepackage[T1]{fontenc}
\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}
\usepackage[margin=0.75in]{geometry}
\usepackage{lmodern}
\usepackage{xcolor}
\usepackage{microtype}
\usepackage{hyperref}
\hypersetup{colorlinks=true,urlcolor=blue!45!black,
  pdftitle={DS675 Milestone 1 - Proposed Datasets},
  pdfauthor={A. Arango-Martinez}}
\setlength{\parindent}{0pt}
\setlength{\parskip}{5pt}
\pagestyle{empty}
\newcommand{\datasetheading}[1]{\vspace{5pt}\textbf{\large #1}\par}

\begin{document}
{\small DS675 \hfill A. Arango-Martinez}\par
{\Large\bfseries Milestone 1: Proposed Datasets}\par
{\normalsize Pressure Prediction in Drinking-Water Distribution Systems}\par
\vspace{2pt}\hrule\vspace{5pt}

\textbf{Area of interest.}
My interest is predicting pressure at remote or critical locations in drinking-water distribution networks. This builds on my previous work on remote-node pressure regulation using field measurements. For this project, I propose supervised regression using current and past SCADA measurements to predict future pressure. Both candidates below contain simulated network data and support a comparison of learning methods beyond exploratory analysis.

\datasetheading{1. BattLeDIM: L-Town}
\textbf{Sources:}
\href{https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/larserikrisholm/battledim}{Kaggle dataset}
\enspace\textbar\enspace
\href{https://zenodo.org/records/4017659}{Official dataset and documentation (Zenodo)}.

\textbf{Description.}
BattLeDIM was developed to evaluate leakage detection and localization in the simulated L-Town network. It provides measurements for 2018 and 2019 at five-minute intervals: 105,120 time observations per year, or 210,240 in total. Its 119 measurement channels comprise 33 pressures, three flows, one tank level, and 82 automated meter readings of consumption. These are numerical time-series variables. Network topology and leakage-event information are also available.

\textbf{Proposed task.}
Predict pressure at a selected monitored node \textbf{15 minutes ahead}. Potential inputs include current and past pressures, flows, tank level, consumption, and time information. The target is a continuous pressure value, so this is regression rather than classification. The node would be selected after reviewing network topology and pressure conditions, with preference for a location relevant to critical-node pressure management.

\textbf{Why consider it?}
Its measurement frequency and distributed sensors closely match my engineering interests, and it has substantially more observations than variables. The original benchmark offers documented prior work on leakage detection; pressure forecasting provides a different project focus. A possible later extension is to examine whether forecast errors change during leakage events.

\datasetheading{2. BATADAL: C-Town}
\textbf{Sources:}
\href{https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/minhbtnguyen/batadal-a-dataset-for-cyber-attack-detection}{Kaggle dataset}
\enspace\textbar\enspace
\href{https://www.batadal.net/data.html}{Official dataset and documentation}.

\textbf{Description.}
BATADAL was developed for cyberattack detection in the simulated C-Town network. The hourly data include 8,761 normal-operation records, 4,177 records in a second development set, and 2,089 test records: 15,027 in total. Its 43 SCADA variables comprise seven tank levels, 12 flows, 12 actuator states, and 12 pressures. Measurements are numerical; actuator states are discrete. Attack information is available separately, with incomplete labeling in the original development data.

\textbf{Proposed task.}
Predict pressure at a selected monitored network location \textbf{one hour ahead}, using current and past tank levels, pump and valve states, flows, and pressures. The target is continuous pressure; attack labels would not be input features or the primary target. Node selection would follow a review of the network and available pressure measurements.

\textbf{Why consider it?}
The combination of hydraulic measurements and actuator states supports studying how network operation relates to future pressure. Its size is manageable and considerably exceeds its feature count. Existing attack-detection work provides background for a different focus on pressure forecasting, potentially followed by analysis under abnormal conditions. Hourly sampling suits operational prediction, rather than fast valve-control dynamics.

\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Scope.}
These are two alternatives; the final dataset remains to be selected. Detailed feature selection and modeling decisions would follow in later milestones. Evaluation would preserve time order and use only information available when each forecast is made.
